Sales and Business Development English

A sales and business development English curriculum for discovery, qualification, objection handling, pricing, procurement, enterprise buying committees, negotiation, partnership language, and CRM discipline.

For account executives, business-development representatives, sales managers, partnership teams, solutions consultants, channel managers, and revenue leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify the workflow, problem, and buying steps without turning a demonstration request into a purchase commitment.
  • Connect a capability to a testable benefit, with a baseline and a distinction between capacity and cash savings.
  • Acknowledge a price concern and compare scope, support, and implementation without inventing competitor claims.
  • Calculate the requested discount and explain its approval status, price base, and review process accurately.
  • Map purchase roles, criteria, and sequence while respecting the main contact and separating interest from authorization.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Discovery and QualificationMake the demonstration relevant before opening the slides+

Your goal

Clarify the workflow, problem, and buying steps without turning a demonstration request into a purchase commitment.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Make the demonstration relevant before opening the slides

Prospect Nina requests a demonstration of an approval-workflow product. Her four branch teams handle about 600 purchase requests per month through spreadsheets and email. She says managers spend time chasing status, but waiting time and the cost of the problem have not been measured. Nina coordinates operations and has not identified the budget holder, approved budget, or purchasing process. Sales representative Caleb must agree a focused demonstration objective and useful discovery steps. Product fit, savings, purchase authority, and a close date are not yet established.

Nina: Prospect operations coordinator / Caleb: Sales representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What current workflow is described?

Answer and explanation

D. About 600 monthly purchase requests across four branches using spreadsheets and email

The brief specifies the volume, branch count, and existing tools without establishing product fit.

2. What is known about the problem?

Answer and explanation

D. Managers chase status, but waiting time and cost have not been measured.

Nina reports a practical problem, while its time and financial effects remain unmeasured.

3. What does the demonstration request establish?

Answer and explanation

D. Interest in seeing the product, not purchase approval

A request to see the product is evidence of interest, not the missing commercial approvals.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

About 600 requests per month describes volume, not delay or savings. Managers chasing status identifies a problem worth examining, but neither the waiting-time baseline nor the cost is supplied. Do not turn those unknowns into a quantified benefit or a guaranteed product result.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
discovery call
A conversation to understand a prospect's situation and needs.conduct a discovery call
qualification
Assessment of whether a sales opportunity has relevant fit and buying conditions.complete opportunity qualification
prospect
A potential customer being considered or engaged.understand the prospect
lead
A potential customer contact or indication of interest under the team's definitions.follow up a lead
use case
A specific situation in which a capability would be used.define the use case
current-state workflow
The sequence of work as it operates now.map the current-state workflow
pain point
A practical problem or difficulty relevant to the customer.clarify the pain point
business impact
The effect of a problem or change on the organization.quantify business impact
discovery gap
Information still missing from understanding the opportunity.identify a discovery gap
ICP
Ideal customer profile; characteristics used to describe a suitable target organization.define the ICP
BANT
Budget, authority, need, and timing; a commonly used qualification checklist.use BANT questions
budget holder
The person or function controlling the relevant funding.identify the budget holder
buying process
The customer's steps for evaluating and authorizing a purchase.clarify the buying process
decision-maker
A person with authority over a relevant purchase decision.identify the decision-maker
end user
A person who will use the product or service.involve end users
technical evaluator
A person assessing relevant technical suitability.engage the technical evaluator
demonstration
A showing of specified product capabilities.tailor the demonstration
demo objective
The question or capability the demonstration should address.agree the demo objective
discovery agenda
The planned topics for the initial needs conversation.agree a discovery agenda
baseline measure
A defined current value used to assess a proposed change.establish a baseline measure
compelling event
A customer-relevant event creating a genuine reason for timing.verify the compelling event
next step
A specific agreed action moving the conversation forward.agree the next step
mutual action plan
A shared sequence of customer and seller actions with owners and dates.develop a mutual action plan
solution fit
How well a proposed offering meets the relevant need and constraints.validate solution fit

Unknown budget is different from confirmed absence of budget. An enthusiastic operations contact is different from a budget holder or authorized signer. Ask how the relevant decisions are made, and record what remains unknown without inventing either approval or rejection.

C Notice the language

Ask with a reason, then listen

A prospect may want to see the product before answering a long list of commercial questions. Explain which few details will make the demonstration relevant and offer a focused agenda. Avoid making unknown budget information sound like a personal failure or treating curiosity as a commitment to buy.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Respect the request
We can show the product; first, may I clarify what would make the demonstration useful?
Ask about the workflow
How does a purchase request move through approval today?
Reflect the current process
Four branches handle about six hundred requests a month through spreadsheets and email.
Clarify the problem
Where do managers lose visibility or need to chase an update?
Avoid a leading conclusion
Is the delay in approval, information gathering, or another step?
Ask about measurement
Do you have a measured waiting-time baseline?
Separate report and estimate
The status-chasing problem is reported; its cost is not yet quantified.
Define the demonstration
Let us focus on routing requests and seeing their current status.
Keep fit provisional
We still need to test whether the workflow fits your requirements.
Clarify the role
Which parts of the evaluation do you coordinate?
Ask about authority respectfully
Who controls the relevant budget and approves a purchase?
Avoid assuming no budget
We have not confirmed the budget position yet.
Understand timing
Is there a customer deadline driving the evaluation?
Avoid a close-date promise
A demonstration does not establish a purchase date.
Agree a useful follow-up
After the demonstration, we can review the fit and remaining questions together.
Close discovery
I will summarize the objective, participants, known facts, and open questions.
6 language and precision notes
May I clarify
Requests cooperation and explains why discovery precedes the demonstration.
About
Preserves the approximate nature of the monthly volume.
Where
Seeks the actual problem location without supplying the answer.
Not yet quantified
Keeps an observed problem distinct from an invented financial result.
Which parts
Clarifies the contact role without challenging personal status.
Open questions
Records uncertainty as work to resolve, not as an assumed refusal.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which demonstration objective fits the stated need?

Answer and explanation

B. Show request routing and status visibility in a relevant workflow.

Routing and status visibility connect directly to the reported workflow and status-chasing problem.

2. Which statement about budget is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. The budget position has not yet been confirmed.

Missing budget information remains unknown rather than proving either available funding or its absence.

3. Which question clarifies the buying process?

Answer and explanation

C. Who evaluates the fit, controls funding, and authorizes the purchase?

All four questions can be useful, but only the first establishes the roles involved in evaluating and authorizing the purchase; the others concern demonstration or workflow discovery.

4. Which benefit statement is supportable now?

Answer and explanation

B. We can examine whether status visibility addresses the problem; savings still need measurement.

The current facts support exploring fit, not a quantified or guaranteed improvement.

E Conversations

Make the demonstration relevant before opening the slides

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nina: Prospect operations coordinator / Caleb: Sales representative

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • demo objective
  • pain point
  • solution fit
  • compelling event
  • use case
  • next step
  • buying process
  • current-state workflow
  • discovery gap
  • baseline measure
  1. Nina
    Can you show us the approval workflow? My managers keep chasing requests through email, and I want to see whether your product makes the status clearer.
  2. Caleb
    Certainly. May I clarify the 1 first so we show the part that matters to your team? A little context will help us focus the session.
  3. Nina
    Four branches handle about six hundred purchase requests a month. People send spreadsheets around and then chase updates by email.
  4. Caleb
    That gives us the 2. Where does visibility break down: when a request is submitted, while it is awaiting approval, or when someone needs more information?
  5. Nina
    I cannot say which step accounts for most of the delay. I know managers spend time asking for status, but we have not measured the waiting time.
  6. Caleb
    Then the 3 is clear enough to explore, while the size and location of the delay remain open. We should not attach a savings figure before understanding those details.
  7. Nina
    We could ask the branch leads for examples of the handoffs. I do not want to send sensitive purchasing records just to arrange a demonstration.
  8. Caleb
    Agreed. We can start with an appropriate process description and a 4 if one is available. Use the approved information-sharing arrangements, and do not send personal or confidential records that we do not need.
  9. Nina
    For the demonstration, I mainly want to see how a manager finds the status and knows whose action is next.
  10. Caleb
    That is a focused 5. We can show request routing and status visibility, then ask whether the example reflects your actual requirements instead of walking through every available feature.
  11. Nina
    I coordinate the operational evaluation. I have not yet involved the people who would assess the system or approve the purchase.
  12. Caleb
    Let us clarify the 6 without assuming your role covers every decision. Who would evaluate technical fit, control funding, and authorize a purchase if the product proves relevant?
  13. Nina
    I need to confirm that internally. I do not have an approved budget figure to give you today.
  14. Caleb
    That remains a 7, not proof that there is no budget. We can record the position accurately and agree how to identify the relevant people rather than fill in assumptions.
  15. Nina
    Just so we are clear, this is an evaluation. I have not promised anyone we will buy this month, and I do not have a purchase date.
  16. Caleb
    Understood. We should not invent a 8 or treat the demonstration as a closing commitment. We can ask about genuine operational deadlines when you have that information.
  17. Nina
    After the demonstration, I would like the branch leads to help identify what fits and what would need further examination.
  18. Caleb
    That is how we can evaluate 9. We should capture both useful capabilities and unresolved requirements, including anything that the demonstration does not establish about your environment.
  19. Nina
    Please send a short agenda with the workflow focus and the questions we should bring to that follow-up.
  20. Caleb
    I will summarize the agreed 10, participants, known facts, and open questions. That will make the demonstration useful without turning your interest into a commitment you have not made.

Answers and explanations
  1. demo objective Turn 2. The objective links the demonstration to the customer's workflow question rather than an unfocused tour of features.
  2. current-state workflow Turn 4. The current-state workflow describes how requests move now, providing context for a relevant product demonstration.
  3. pain point Turn 6. The pain point is status chasing; the location and measured size of the delay remain to be established.
  4. baseline measure Turn 8. A baseline measure establishes current performance; request volume alone does not quantify waiting time or savings.
  5. use case Turn 10. The use case is routing and status visibility in the approval workflow, not every capability the product offers.
  6. buying process Turn 12. The buying process identifies evaluation and approval steps that Nina's demonstration request does not establish.
  7. discovery gap Turn 14. A discovery gap is missing information, not proof that funding is either approved or unavailable.
  8. compelling event Turn 16. A compelling event is a genuine customer timing driver, not a seller-created date inferred from interest.
  9. solution fit Turn 18. Solution fit needs assessment against actual requirements; a successful demonstration alone does not establish it.
  10. next step Turn 20. The next step is a focused agenda and follow-up, not an invented purchase approval or close date.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nina: Can you show us the approval workflow? My managers keep chasing requests through email, and I want to see whether your product makes the status clearer.
  2. Caleb: Certainly. May I clarify the demo objective first so we show the part that matters to your team? A little context will help us focus the session.
  3. Nina: Four branches handle about six hundred purchase requests a month. People send spreadsheets around and then chase updates by email.
  4. Caleb: That gives us the current-state workflow. Where does visibility break down: when a request is submitted, while it is awaiting approval, or when someone needs more information?
  5. Nina: I cannot say which step accounts for most of the delay. I know managers spend time asking for status, but we have not measured the waiting time.
  6. Caleb: Then the pain point is clear enough to explore, while the size and location of the delay remain open. We should not attach a savings figure before understanding those details.
  7. Nina: We could ask the branch leads for examples of the handoffs. I do not want to send sensitive purchasing records just to arrange a demonstration.
  8. Caleb: Agreed. We can start with an appropriate process description and a baseline measure if one is available. Use the approved information-sharing arrangements, and do not send personal or confidential records that we do not need.
  9. Nina: For the demonstration, I mainly want to see how a manager finds the status and knows whose action is next.
  10. Caleb: That is a focused use case. We can show request routing and status visibility, then ask whether the example reflects your actual requirements instead of walking through every available feature.
  11. Nina: I coordinate the operational evaluation. I have not yet involved the people who would assess the system or approve the purchase.
  12. Caleb: Let us clarify the buying process without assuming your role covers every decision. Who would evaluate technical fit, control funding, and authorize a purchase if the product proves relevant?
  13. Nina: I need to confirm that internally. I do not have an approved budget figure to give you today.
  14. Caleb: That remains a discovery gap, not proof that there is no budget. We can record the position accurately and agree how to identify the relevant people rather than fill in assumptions.
  15. Nina: Just so we are clear, this is an evaluation. I have not promised anyone we will buy this month, and I do not have a purchase date.
  16. Caleb: Understood. We should not invent a compelling event or treat the demonstration as a closing commitment. We can ask about genuine operational deadlines when you have that information.
  17. Nina: After the demonstration, I would like the branch leads to help identify what fits and what would need further examination.
  18. Caleb: That is how we can evaluate solution fit. We should capture both useful capabilities and unresolved requirements, including anything that the demonstration does not establish about your environment.
  19. Nina: Please send a short agenda with the workflow focus and the questions we should bring to that follow-up.
  20. Caleb: I will summarize the agreed next step, participants, known facts, and open questions. That will make the demonstration useful without turning your interest into a commitment you have not made.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A demonstration needs a workflow

The lesson's prospect requests a demonstration without explaining the workflow, problem, or buying process.

  1. Account executive: Before arranging the demonstration, could you describe the workflow you want us to focus on?
  2. Prospect operations lead: We haven't shared that yet. I don't want a general tour that misses our actual problem.
  3. Account executive: Which step currently causes the most difficulty, and who experiences that pain point?
  4. Prospect operations lead: I need to consult the users before answering precisely. My initial request was exploratory.
  5. Account executive: That's useful to know. Discovery can clarify the need before we assume a particular use case fits.
  6. Prospect operations lead: Would you also need to know our buying process before showing us anything?
  7. Account executive: We can hold an exploratory discussion, but we shouldn't mistake interest in a demonstration for a qualified purchase.
  8. Prospect operations lead: Understood. I'll identify the workflow owner and ask what they want to examine.
  9. Account executive: Thank you. We can then agree a focused demonstration and clarify the decision process at an appropriate stage.
  10. Prospect operations lead: Let's start there, without treating the meeting request as a commitment to buy.
A replacement request with an unclear trigger

Separate fictional case: a prospect asks about replacing a scheduling system, but has not said whether the concern is cost, usability, or a contract event.

  1. Business development representative: You mentioned replacing your scheduling system. Could you clarify what prompted you to explore alternatives now?
  2. Prospect service manager: Different teams have concerns, but we haven't agreed which issue is the main reason for considering a change.
  3. Business development representative: Then I shouldn't assume the pain point is pricing simply because you asked for a quote.
  4. Prospect service manager: Correct. The quote helps us explore, but it won't define what a replacement needs to improve.
  5. Business development representative: Who could explain a typical scheduling problem and the effect it has on the service team?
  6. Prospect service manager: I'll involve the scheduling supervisor. They can describe actual examples rather than general dissatisfaction.
  7. Business development representative: Good. Could we also clarify whether there is a buying deadline, rather than infer one from this inquiry?
  8. Prospect service manager: There isn't a confirmed date in the information I've shared. I'll check the internal planning process.
  9. Business development representative: I'll record that as unknown and keep the next discussion focused on requirements and qualification.
  10. Prospect service manager: That works. We need clarity before we compare replacements or imply that procurement has begun.
A trial request from outside the user team

Separate fictional case: an innovation manager requests a trial for a warehouse workflow, but the warehouse owner has not participated in discovery.

  1. Account executive: Could you explain which warehouse task the proposed trial should test and who owns that workflow?
  2. Prospect innovation manager: I requested the trial, but the warehouse owner hasn't joined the discussion yet. The task is still broad.
  3. Account executive: Then we should involve that owner before treating the trial as evidence of an agreed operational need.
  4. Prospect innovation manager: Could we run it with my team first to demonstrate momentum, even without their input?
  5. Account executive: We could discuss a limited exploration, but it wouldn't validate warehouse fit unless the relevant workflow is represented.
  6. Prospect innovation manager: I understand. Interest from innovation isn't the same as qualification with the people who would use it.
  7. Account executive: Exactly. Could you arrange a conversation about the current process, pain point, and intended decision?
  8. Prospect innovation manager: I'll ask the warehouse owner to participate and confirm capacity to assess a trial.
  9. Account executive: Thank you. We can then discuss scope without assuming a purchase decision or implementation commitment.
  10. Prospect innovation manager: I'll present the next meeting as discovery, not as an approved trial launch.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Make the demonstration relevant before opening the slides
  1. Read Nina and Caleb aloud, then swap roles. Ask for context without making the demonstration conditional on immediate budget answers.
  2. Check all ten gaps. Reread the measured-baseline and buying-role exchanges without inventing missing information.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Qualify another demonstration request

An operations contact asks to see a scheduling product. Three sites currently coordinate by email. Missed updates are reported, but their frequency, budget, approver, and purchase date are unconfirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Seller: "The existing coordination tool is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. email

The brief identifies email as the current method across the three sites.

2. Contact: "The reported problem is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. missed updates

Missed updates are reported, but their frequency and impact have not been measured.

3. Seller: "The relevant number of sites is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. three

Three sites define the stated operational footprint for this initial discussion.

4. Contact: "The purchase date is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unconfirmed

A request to see the product does not establish a buying commitment or date.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Discovery and Qualification
Original fictional case: Prospect Nina requests a demonstration of an approval-workflow product. Her four branch teams handle about 600 purchase requests per month through spreadsheets and email. She says managers spend time chasing status, but waiting time and the cost of the problem have not been measured. Nina coordinates operations and has not identified the budget holder, approved budget, or purchasing process. Sales representative Caleb must agree a focused demonstration objective and useful discovery steps. Product fit, savings, purchase authority, and a close date are not yet established.
Language workshop: Make the demonstration relevant before opening the slides
Communication goal: Clarify the workflow, problem, and buying steps without turning a demonstration request into a purchase commitment.
Language guidance: About 600 requests per month describes volume, not delay or savings. Managers chasing status identifies a problem worth examining, but neither the waiting-time baseline nor the cost is supplied. Do not turn those unknowns into a quantified benefit or a guaranteed product result.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We can show the product; first, may I clarify what would make the demonstration useful? / How does a purchase request move through approval today? / Four branches handle about six hundred requests a month through spreadsheets and email. / Where do managers lose visibility or need to chase an update? / Is the delay in approval, information gathering, or another step? / Do you have a measured waiting-time baseline? / The status-chasing problem is reported; its cost is not yet quantified. / Let us focus on routing requests and seeing their current status. / We still need to test whether the workflow fits your requirements. / Which parts of the evaluation do you coordinate? / Who controls the relevant budget and approves a purchase? / We have not confirmed the budget position yet. / Is there a customer deadline driving the evaluation? / A demonstration does not establish a purchase date. / After the demonstration, we can review the fit and remaining questions together. / I will summarize the objective, participants, known facts, and open questions.
Vocabulary: discovery call: A conversation to understand a prospect's situation and needs. / qualification: Assessment of whether a sales opportunity has relevant fit and buying conditions. / prospect: A potential customer being considered or engaged. / lead: A potential customer contact or indication of interest under the team's definitions. / use case: A specific situation in which a capability would be used. / current-state workflow: The sequence of work as it operates now. / pain point: A practical problem or difficulty relevant to the customer. / business impact: The effect of a problem or change on the organization. / discovery gap: Information still missing from understanding the opportunity. / ICP: Ideal customer profile; characteristics used to describe a suitable target organization. / BANT: Budget, authority, need, and timing; a commonly used qualification checklist. / budget holder: The person or function controlling the relevant funding. / buying process: The customer's steps for evaluating and authorizing a purchase. / decision-maker: A person with authority over a relevant purchase decision. / end user: A person who will use the product or service. / technical evaluator: A person assessing relevant technical suitability. / demonstration: A showing of specified product capabilities. / demo objective: The question or capability the demonstration should address. / discovery agenda: The planned topics for the initial needs conversation. / baseline measure: A defined current value used to assess a proposed change. / compelling event: A customer-relevant event creating a genuine reason for timing. / next step: A specific agreed action moving the conversation forward. / mutual action plan: A shared sequence of customer and seller actions with owners and dates. / solution fit: How well a proposed offering meets the relevant need and constraints.
Speaking task: Read Nina and Caleb aloud, then swap roles. Ask for context without making the demonstration conditional on immediate budget answers. Check all ten gaps. Reread the measured-baseline and buying-role exchanges without inventing missing information. Repeat the agreed demonstration agenda and follow-up, keeping product interest distinct from a purchase commitment.
Partner: Nina: Prospect operations coordinator / Caleb: Sales representative
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An operations contact asks to see a scheduling product. Three sites currently coordinate by email. Missed updates are reported, but their frequency, budget, approver, and purchase date are unconfirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nina: After the demonstration, I would like the branch leads to help identify what fits and what would need further examination.
Caleb: That is how we can evaluate solution fit. We should capture both useful capabilities and unresolved requirements, including anything that the demonstration does not establish about your environment.
Nina: Please send a short agenda with the workflow focus and the questions we should bring to that follow-up.
Caleb: I will summarize the agreed next step, participants, known facts, and open questions. That will make the demonstration useful without turning your interest into a commitment you have not made.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 01: Discovery and Qualification
Original fictional case: Prospect Nina requests a demonstration of an approval-workflow product. Her four branch teams handle about 600 purchase requests per month through spreadsheets and email. She says managers spend time chasing status, but waiting time and the cost of the problem have not been measured. Nina coordinates operations and has not identified the budget holder, approved budget, or purchasing process. Sales representative Caleb must agree a focused demonstration objective and useful discovery steps. Product fit, savings, purchase authority, and a close date are not yet established.
Language workshop: Make the demonstration relevant before opening the slides
Communication goal: Clarify the workflow, problem, and buying steps without turning a demonstration request into a purchase commitment.
Language guidance: About 600 requests per month describes volume, not delay or savings. Managers chasing status identifies a problem worth examining, but neither the waiting-time baseline nor the cost is supplied. Do not turn those unknowns into a quantified benefit or a guaranteed product result.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We can show the product; first, may I clarify what would make the demonstration useful? / How does a purchase request move through approval today? / Four branches handle about six hundred requests a month through spreadsheets and email. / Where do managers lose visibility or need to chase an update? / Is the delay in approval, information gathering, or another step? / Do you have a measured waiting-time baseline? / The status-chasing problem is reported; its cost is not yet quantified. / Let us focus on routing requests and seeing their current status. / We still need to test whether the workflow fits your requirements. / Which parts of the evaluation do you coordinate? / Who controls the relevant budget and approves a purchase? / We have not confirmed the budget position yet. / Is there a customer deadline driving the evaluation? / A demonstration does not establish a purchase date. / After the demonstration, we can review the fit and remaining questions together. / I will summarize the objective, participants, known facts, and open questions.
Vocabulary: discovery call: A conversation to understand a prospect's situation and needs. / qualification: Assessment of whether a sales opportunity has relevant fit and buying conditions. / prospect: A potential customer being considered or engaged. / lead: A potential customer contact or indication of interest under the team's definitions. / use case: A specific situation in which a capability would be used. / current-state workflow: The sequence of work as it operates now. / pain point: A practical problem or difficulty relevant to the customer. / business impact: The effect of a problem or change on the organization. / discovery gap: Information still missing from understanding the opportunity. / ICP: Ideal customer profile; characteristics used to describe a suitable target organization. / BANT: Budget, authority, need, and timing; a commonly used qualification checklist. / budget holder: The person or function controlling the relevant funding. / buying process: The customer's steps for evaluating and authorizing a purchase. / decision-maker: A person with authority over a relevant purchase decision. / end user: A person who will use the product or service. / technical evaluator: A person assessing relevant technical suitability. / demonstration: A showing of specified product capabilities. / demo objective: The question or capability the demonstration should address. / discovery agenda: The planned topics for the initial needs conversation. / baseline measure: A defined current value used to assess a proposed change. / compelling event: A customer-relevant event creating a genuine reason for timing. / next step: A specific agreed action moving the conversation forward. / mutual action plan: A shared sequence of customer and seller actions with owners and dates. / solution fit: How well a proposed offering meets the relevant need and constraints.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nina: After the demonstration, I would like the branch leads to help identify what fits and what would need further examination.
Caleb: That is how we can evaluate solution fit. We should capture both useful capabilities and unresolved requirements, including anything that the demonstration does not establish about your environment.
Nina: Please send a short agenda with the workflow focus and the questions we should bring to that follow-up.
Caleb: I will summarize the agreed next step, participants, known facts, and open questions. That will make the demonstration useful without turning your interest into a commitment you have not made.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. 02Value Proposition and Use-Case FitConnect the feature to the reporting work it might change+

Your goal

Connect a capability to a testable benefit, with a baseline and a distinction between capacity and cash savings.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Connect the feature to the reporting work it might change

Evan spends 24 staff hours preparing each monthly report: 14 consolidating inputs, 8 reviewing them, and 2 formatting the output. Sales representative Sera demonstrates automated consolidation using sample data. Evan proposes testing whether consolidation could fall from 14 to 6 hours while review and formatting remain unchanged. That would reduce total preparation to 16 hours, freeing 8 hours per cycle. No customer-data test has occurred, compatibility is unverified, and no staffing cost reduction is planned. Sera must describe a value hypothesis, not guaranteed savings or a proven return on investment.

Evan: Prospect reporting lead / Sera: Sales representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How is the current 24-hour cycle divided?

Answer and explanation

A. 14 hours consolidation, 8 review, and 2 formatting

The three supplied work categories sum to twenty-four staff hours per monthly cycle.

2. What total follows from the proposed test assumption?

Answer and explanation

C. 16 hours, if consolidation falls to 6 and the other tasks stay unchanged

Six consolidation hours plus eight review and two formatting hours would total sixteen.

3. What has actually been demonstrated?

Answer and explanation

C. Consolidation with sample data, not verified customer results

The demonstration shows a capability on sample data, while customer fit and benefits remain unverified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Under the proposed assumption, consolidation falls by 8 hours, and total preparation falls from 24 to 16. The total time reduction would be 8 divided by 24, approximately 33.3%. The demonstration has not established that this reduction will occur in the customer workflow.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
value proposition
A clear explanation of the relevant benefit an offering is proposed to provide.tailor the value proposition
feature
A specific capability or characteristic of a product.demonstrate a feature
feature-to-value link
The explanation connecting a capability to a customer outcome.explain the feature-to-value link
value hypothesis
A proposed customer benefit to be tested against evidence.validate the value hypothesis
business outcome
A result meaningful to the customer's operation or goals.define the business outcome
quantified benefit
A proposed or observed improvement expressed in a defined measure.substantiate a quantified benefit
business case
An assessment of expected value, costs, conditions, and risks for a decision.build the business case
proof of value
A bounded evaluation of whether a solution produces a relevant customer benefit.agree a proof of value
time-to-value
The period before a defined benefit is achieved.estimate time-to-value
value realization
Actual achievement of the intended benefit.track value realization
workflow fit
Compatibility between the solution and the customer's actual work.test workflow fit
manual consolidation
Combining information through human effort rather than the proposed automation.reduce manual consolidation
data readiness
Whether data meets the conditions needed for the proposed use.assess data readiness
compatibility
Ability of components or data to work together as required.verify compatibility
configuration
Settings or arrangements used to adapt a product to the required use.confirm the configuration
integration
Connection between systems or processes to support the intended workflow.scope the integration
exception handling
Work to address items that do not follow the normal path.assess exception handling
representative dataset
Data selected to reflect the relevant real-world conditions for a test.use a representative dataset
acceptance criterion
A defined condition for judging the agreed test or output.set acceptance criteria
benefit owner
The person responsible for confirming and tracking a particular benefit.identify the benefit owner
baseline
The defined current position used for comparison.confirm the baseline
labor capacity
Staff time or capability available for work.release labor capacity
cash saving
A reduction in actual cash expenditure under a specified comparison.distinguish cash savings
assumption
A condition taken as true for a calculation until verified.disclose the assumptions

Freed staff time is potential capacity, not automatically lower payroll or cash expenditure. No staffing cost reduction is planned here. A return-on-investment calculation would also need relevant costs, timing, and a justified benefit measure that the brief does not supply.

C Notice the language

The customer outcome comes before the feature tour

A technically impressive feature may not answer the concern of the person buying it. Ask which work changes, what remains, and how the result will be checked. A credible explanation of limits can be more persuasive than a long list of capabilities detached from the customer workflow.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with the workflow
Which part of preparing the report consumes the most time?
State the baseline
The current cycle takes twenty-four staff hours.
Link the capability
Automated consolidation addresses the fourteen-hour input-combining step.
Keep other work visible
Review and formatting do not disappear in this proposal.
State the hypothesis
We would test whether consolidation can fall to six hours.
Calculate conditionally
If the other tasks stay unchanged, total preparation would be sixteen hours.
Name the potential capacity
That would free eight staff hours per monthly cycle.
Avoid a guaranteed claim
The customer-data test has not yet established that saving.
Separate demonstration and proof
Sample-data performance does not confirm compatibility with your inputs.
Define a useful test
Use an approved representative dataset and agreed success criteria.
Check exceptions
Include the unusual inputs that currently require manual attention.
Assign benefit ownership
Who will verify the before-and-after time measurement?
Distinguish value types
Released capacity is not automatically a cash saving.
Avoid invented return
We cannot state a return on investment without the relevant costs and benefit assumptions.
State the implementation dependency
Configuration and integration requirements still need assessment.
Close the value discussion
Let us agree what to test, how to measure it, and what the result would establish.
6 language and precision notes
Addresses
Links a feature to a particular work step without claiming every task disappears.
Would test
Marks the benefit as a hypothesis.
If
Makes the calculation conditional on the stated assumptions.
Per monthly cycle
Keeps the time-saving unit explicit.
Not automatically
Separates released time from a reduction in expenditure.
What the result would establish
Defines the claim that a bounded test could legitimately support.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which value claim is accurate before the customer-data test?

Answer and explanation

C. The proposed change could free eight hours per cycle if the stated assumptions hold.

The conditional claim follows the supplied arithmetic while preserving the unverified customer result.

2. Why is the proposed total sixteen rather than six hours?

Answer and explanation

D. Review and formatting still require ten hours combined.

The six-hour consolidation assumption must be added to eight review hours and two formatting hours.

3. What should a relevant proof of value include?

Answer and explanation

B. Approved representative inputs, exceptions, measures, and agreed criteria

A meaningful test needs representative work and agreed measures. Easy samples, omitted correction time, and satisfaction alone cannot establish the claimed reduction in the customer's full reporting cycle.

4. Which distinction matters for the business case?

Answer and explanation

A. Freed staff capacity does not necessarily reduce cash spending.

Capacity can be useful without producing a cash reduction, so the benefit measure must be stated accurately.

Evan: Prospect reporting lead / Sera: Sales representative

Answers and explanations
  1. value proposition Turn 2. The value proposition should address the customer's reporting outcome, rather than merely list product capabilities.
  2. baseline Turn 4. The baseline is the current twenty-four-hour cycle, with the three work categories separately defined.
  3. value hypothesis Turn 6. The proposed six-hour consolidation result is a benefit hypothesis to test, not an achieved customer outcome.
  4. workflow fit Turn 8. Workflow fit depends on the real inputs and exceptions, which a smooth sample demonstration does not establish.
  5. representative dataset Turn 10. Representative data should reflect relevant branch formats and exceptions, rather than only easy records selected for a demonstration.
  6. proof of value Turn 12. Proof of value tests the proposed customer benefit against agreed measures and scope, rather than assuming demonstration success transfers automatically.
  7. labor capacity Turn 14. Labor capacity is staff time available for other work; no payroll or cash reduction is planned here.
  8. assumption Turn 16. The approximate one-third reduction depends on the unverified six-hour consolidation result and unchanged review and formatting work.
  9. integration Turn 18. Integration requirements are implementation dependencies that must be assessed before assuming the demonstrated capability works in the customer environment.
  10. benefit owner Turn 20. The benefit owner verifies and tracks the actual outcome, keeping responsibility separate from a seller's untested value claim.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Features are not the prospect's outcome

The lesson's prospect wants less time spent preparing monthly reports, but the sales slide lists features.

  1. Prospect reporting manager: Your slide lists features, but I need to reduce the time we spend preparing monthly reports.
  2. Solutions consultant: You're asking about an outcome, not a feature list. We should connect a relevant use case to that goal.
  3. Prospect reporting manager: Could you explain the difference using our reporting need rather than another broad product description?
  4. Solutions consultant: A feature is something the product does; a value proposition explains how that capability could help your workflow.
  5. Prospect reporting manager: So faster report preparation is the intended benefit, but we still need evidence that it happens here.
  6. Solutions consultant: Exactly. We haven't measured your baseline or validated a time-saving result in your process.
  7. Prospect reporting manager: Then the success metric should concern preparation time using a consistent definition, not the number of features shown.
  8. Solutions consultant: Agreed. We should involve the stakeholders who perform and review monthly reporting.
  9. Prospect reporting manager: Let's clarify that workflow before using any percentage-saving claim in the proposal.
  10. Solutions consultant: I'll focus the next discussion on your use case, measurement boundaries, and what remains to be tested.
A notification is not a resolved approval

Separate fictional case: a prospect wants fewer delayed approvals; a demonstration shows automatic notifications, but no outcome test has occurred.

  1. Sales engineer: The demonstration shows an automatic notification. It doesn't yet establish that your approval delays will decrease.
  2. Prospect process owner: I assumed sending reminders was the same thing as solving the delay. What distinction should we consider?
  3. Sales engineer: A notification prompts someone; it doesn't ensure they have the information, time, or authority to approve.
  4. Prospect process owner: Then our use case needs to explain what causes the waiting, rather than count messages sent.
  5. Sales engineer: Exactly. The value proposition should connect the capability to your actual obstacle, with assumptions made explicit.
  6. Prospect process owner: Would time from a complete request to a decision be a more relevant success metric?
  7. Sales engineer: It could be, once we agree the definition and check whether the necessary data is available.
  8. Prospect process owner: We should also include the approvers, because their part of the workflow determines whether a reminder is useful.
  9. Sales engineer: Agreed. I'll avoid claiming a measured reduction until a suitable evaluation supports it.
  10. Prospect process owner: Let's assess the approval workflow first and keep the notification as a capability, not a promised outcome.
One export button, several reporting responsibilities

Separate fictional case: a sales demo shows a one-click export; the prospect's reporting workflow also requires review and reconciliation, which have not been assessed.

  1. Prospect finance operations lead: The export takes one click. Does that mean our entire monthly reporting task becomes a one-click process?
  2. Account executive: No. The demonstration covers export only; your review and reconciliation steps have not been assessed.
  3. Prospect finance operations lead: Then the feature shortens one visible action, but the value claim needs the whole use case.
  4. Account executive: Exactly. We should distinguish a demonstrated capability from the time required to produce an accepted report.
  5. Prospect finance operations lead: Could the success metric include all preparation and review time, rather than only the export duration?
  6. Account executive: Yes, if we agree consistent boundaries and measure them. We shouldn't exclude work merely because it occurs outside the product.
  7. Prospect finance operations lead: That would give our reporting stakeholders a more realistic basis for assessing the benefit.
  8. Account executive: I'll describe the export accurately and ask which remaining steps a pilot needs to examine.
  9. Prospect finance operations lead: Please keep any end-to-end saving as unverified until those steps are included.
  10. Account executive: Agreed. The proposal will separate what we demonstrated from what your workflow still needs to validate.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Connect the feature to the reporting work it might change
  1. Read Evan and Sera aloud, then swap roles. Give the fourteen, eight, and two hours as separate parts of the current cycle.
  2. Check the ten gaps. Reread the conditional sixteen-hour total and eight-hour capacity benefit.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain another conditional time benefit

A task takes 18 hours: 10 combining inputs, 6 reviewing, and 2 formatting. A proposed test targets 4 hours for combining, with the other work unchanged. No test result or staffing cost reduction exists.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Seller: "The current total is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 18 hours

Ten combining hours plus six review and two formatting hours equals eighteen.

2. Buyer: "The proposed combining time is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 4 hours

Four hours is the test target, not a result already demonstrated.

3. Seller: "The conditional new total would be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 12 hours

Four combining hours plus six review and two formatting hours would total twelve.

4. Buyer: "The potential time released per cycle is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 6 hours

Eighteen minus twelve gives six hours of potential capacity, not an automatic cash saving.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Value Proposition and Use-Case Fit
Original fictional case: Evan spends 24 staff hours preparing each monthly report: 14 consolidating inputs, 8 reviewing them, and 2 formatting the output. Sales representative Sera demonstrates automated consolidation using sample data. Evan proposes testing whether consolidation could fall from 14 to 6 hours while review and formatting remain unchanged. That would reduce total preparation to 16 hours, freeing 8 hours per cycle. No customer-data test has occurred, compatibility is unverified, and no staffing cost reduction is planned. Sera must describe a value hypothesis, not guaranteed savings or a proven return on investment.
Language workshop: Connect the feature to the reporting work it might change
Communication goal: Connect a capability to a testable benefit, with a baseline and a distinction between capacity and cash savings.
Language guidance: Under the proposed assumption, consolidation falls by 8 hours, and total preparation falls from 24 to 16. The total time reduction would be 8 divided by 24, approximately 33.3%. The demonstration has not established that this reduction will occur in the customer workflow.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which part of preparing the report consumes the most time? / The current cycle takes twenty-four staff hours. / Automated consolidation addresses the fourteen-hour input-combining step. / Review and formatting do not disappear in this proposal. / We would test whether consolidation can fall to six hours. / If the other tasks stay unchanged, total preparation would be sixteen hours. / That would free eight staff hours per monthly cycle. / The customer-data test has not yet established that saving. / Sample-data performance does not confirm compatibility with your inputs. / Use an approved representative dataset and agreed success criteria. / Include the unusual inputs that currently require manual attention. / Who will verify the before-and-after time measurement? / Released capacity is not automatically a cash saving. / We cannot state a return on investment without the relevant costs and benefit assumptions. / Configuration and integration requirements still need assessment. / Let us agree what to test, how to measure it, and what the result would establish.
Vocabulary: value proposition: A clear explanation of the relevant benefit an offering is proposed to provide. / feature: A specific capability or characteristic of a product. / feature-to-value link: The explanation connecting a capability to a customer outcome. / value hypothesis: A proposed customer benefit to be tested against evidence. / business outcome: A result meaningful to the customer's operation or goals. / quantified benefit: A proposed or observed improvement expressed in a defined measure. / business case: An assessment of expected value, costs, conditions, and risks for a decision. / proof of value: A bounded evaluation of whether a solution produces a relevant customer benefit. / time-to-value: The period before a defined benefit is achieved. / value realization: Actual achievement of the intended benefit. / workflow fit: Compatibility between the solution and the customer's actual work. / manual consolidation: Combining information through human effort rather than the proposed automation. / data readiness: Whether data meets the conditions needed for the proposed use. / compatibility: Ability of components or data to work together as required. / configuration: Settings or arrangements used to adapt a product to the required use. / integration: Connection between systems or processes to support the intended workflow. / exception handling: Work to address items that do not follow the normal path. / representative dataset: Data selected to reflect the relevant real-world conditions for a test. / acceptance criterion: A defined condition for judging the agreed test or output. / benefit owner: The person responsible for confirming and tracking a particular benefit. / baseline: The defined current position used for comparison. / labor capacity: Staff time or capability available for work. / cash saving: A reduction in actual cash expenditure under a specified comparison. / assumption: A condition taken as true for a calculation until verified.
Speaking task: Read Evan and Sera aloud, then swap roles. Give the fourteen, eight, and two hours as separate parts of the current cycle. Check the ten gaps. Reread the conditional sixteen-hour total and eight-hour capacity benefit. Repeat the proposed test close with representative inputs, exceptions, timing measures, and a benefit owner; retain the distinction between capacity and cash.
Partner: Evan: Prospect reporting lead / Sera: Sales representative
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A task takes 18 hours: 10 combining inputs, 6 reviewing, and 2 formatting. A proposed test targets 4 hours for combining, with the other work unchanged. No test result or staffing cost reduction exists.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Evan: We also need to understand what configuration or connections would be required before our team could use this in a real monthly cycle.
Sera: Those integration requirements still need assessment, along with configuration and data readiness. I cannot give a reliable time-to-value or return calculation without the relevant implementation conditions and costs.
Evan: I will be responsible for verifying whether the measured reporting work actually changes. We can review the outcome with the team after the evaluation.
Sera: Good; naming the benefit owner makes the next step clear. Let us agree the test scope, representative inputs, measures, and criteria, then use the result to support a proportionate claim about the value.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
