Course: Sales and Business Development English
Professional audience: account executives, business-development representatives, sales managers, partnership teams, solutions consultants, channel managers, and revenue leaders
Scope: All customers, products, people, quotes, contracts, prices, policies, and timelines are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal, financial, procurement, or product advice. Follow the actual evidence, approved claims, commercial authority, and contract-review process. FTC references concern United States advertising guidance; they are not a complete statement of applicable law. No cited organization endorses this book.
Lesson 06: Negotiation and Contract Redlines
Original fictional case: Buyer Priya returns agreement version 4 with a proposed change to clause 10.2, removing a stated liability cap and requesting uncapped liability. She asks seller Marcus to accept it today. The company requires legal review and authorized commercial approval for this change; Marcus holds neither authority. No response wording has been approved. Marcus must preserve the marked document, clarify the business concern for counsel, and track the review. He promises a status update at 16:00 Eastern today, not acceptance. The effect of the clause depends on the complete agreement and relevant law, which the exercise does not determine.
Language workshop: A customer redline needs review, not an immediate yes
Communication goal: Route the exact proposed contract change for review without accepting it or promising a legal outcome.
Language guidance: Receiving a marked agreement means a proposal has arrived; it does not mean the changed clause is accepted. A clean version can also contain unapproved wording. Keep document status, review status, commercial approval, and signature status distinct.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have received your marked version four. / The proposed change is to clause ten point two. / You are asking to remove the stated liability cap. / I am acknowledging the proposal, not accepting the wording. / This change requires legal review and authorized commercial approval. / I cannot determine the clause's effect or enforceability. / What business concern should I include when routing the request to counsel? / I will send the full marked agreement, not a paraphrase of the clause alone. / The reviewers need the complete document and relevant cross-references. / No alternative wording has been approved for this request yet. / Any response should identify the version and changes clearly. / I understand the deadline, but it does not change the required authority. / I will update you at sixteen hundred Eastern today. / That update may report that the review is still pending. / The final clean text must match the authorized negotiated position. / The proposed change remains under review until the required decision is communicated.
Vocabulary: redline: A document showing proposed changes to existing wording. / markup: Visible additions, deletions, or comments in a document. / clean version: A version without visible change marks, whose approval status still needs checking. / version control: Identification and management of document revisions. / contract clause: A defined provision within an agreement. / liability cap: A stated limit on specified liability, subject to the wording and applicable law. / uncapped liability: Liability without the specified contractual cap, subject to the full legal context. / indemnity: An obligation concerning compensation for specified losses under the relevant terms. / warranty: A contractual assurance with meaning and remedies determined by the relevant terms and law. / representation: A statement of fact whose legal effect depends on context and applicable law. / governing law: The law identified or determined as applicable to an agreement or issue. / jurisdiction: The relevant authority or forum's legal power over a matter. / carve-out: An exception to a stated contractual rule or limitation. / consequential loss: A category of loss whose scope depends on applicable law and contract interpretation. / third-party claim: A claim made by someone outside the contracting parties. / MSA: Master services agreement; overarching terms governing specified services or later orders. / order form: A document setting out the specific purchase and related terms. / DPA: Data processing agreement or addendum governing specified processing arrangements. / approved fallback: Alternative wording authorized for defined circumstances. / escalation: Routing an issue to the appropriate authority or specialist. / authorized signatory: A person empowered to sign the relevant agreement. / signature workflow: The agreed steps for approving and executing the contract. / negotiating position: A proposed stance on terms, distinct from a final agreement. / acceptance: Assent to proposed terms, with legal effect determined by the relevant circumstances.
Speaking task: Read Priya and Marcus aloud, then swap roles. Acknowledge the urgent deadline without sounding as though the clause has been accepted. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the exact-version, full-markup, and authorized-review exchanges. Repeat the sixteen-hundred Eastern status commitment, keeping the review outcome and eventual signature separate.
Partner: Priya: Buyer coordinating contract comments / Marcus: Account executive
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A buyer returns version 7 with a change to clause 8.3. The account executive cannot accept it and must obtain legal and authorized commercial review. The next status update is due at 11:00 Central.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: Our team will need a clean document for signature eventually, but I do not want clean formatting to hide a disagreement.
Marcus: Exactly. A clean version must be reconciled with the authorized position. Removing markup is a formatting step, not evidence that the underlying terms have been approved.
Priya: Please confirm the signing route once the wording is actually agreed through the required process.
Marcus: We will verify the authorized signatory and signature workflow then. For now, the proposed clause change remains under review, and my next commitment is the status update at sixteen hundred Eastern.
END REFERENCE
07Partnerships and Channel DevelopmentDefine the partnership before promising exclusivity+

Your goal

Define territory, products, duration, contribution, and funding before promising partnership rights.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Define the partnership before promising exclusivity

Chen requests exclusivity without defining territory, products, duration, or contribution. Amina cannot grant it. For assessment, the team proposes a six-month nonexclusive scheduling-product pilot in fictional Region North, targeting four qualified opportunities per quarter. Qualification requires a customer need, relevant contact, and agreed next step, not an order. Chen requests $5,000 marketing support; none is approved. All pilot terms require commercial and legal review.

Chen: Potential channel partner / Amina: Channel development manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is missing from the initial exclusivity request?

Answer and explanation

C. Defined territory, products, duration, and contribution

The initial request leaves the scope and contribution undefined, so it is not a complete proposed arrangement.

2. What pilot is being considered?

Answer and explanation

B. A six-month nonexclusive scheduling-product pilot in Region North

The brief specifies a bounded nonexclusive pilot for assessment, while all terms remain unapproved.

3. What does four qualified opportunities per quarter mean here?

Answer and explanation

D. Four opportunities meeting the stated need, contact, and next-step criteria

The supplied qualification definition concerns opportunity evidence, not completed purchases or revenue.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A qualified opportunity is not a signed order. This pilot proposal uses three explicit conditions: identified need, relevant contact, and agreed next step. Four opportunities per quarter would measure pipeline contribution under that definition, not guaranteed bookings or cash receipts.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
channel partner
An organization helping market, sell, deliver, or support an offering under agreed terms.develop a channel partner
referral partner
A partner introducing potential customers under the relevant arrangement.define a referral partnership
reseller
A party selling another organization's offering under agreed rights and terms.appoint a reseller
distributor
An intermediary supplying products to other sellers or channels under an arrangement.work with a distributor
VAR
Value-added reseller; a reseller combining an offering with additional services or capabilities.assess a VAR model
MSP
Managed service provider; a provider operating specified services for customers.engage an MSP
ISV
Independent software vendor; a company developing and selling its own software.coordinate an ISV partnership
co-selling
Seller and partner working together on a defined sales opportunity.agree co-selling roles
partner enablement
Training, resources, and support preparing partners to perform agreed activities.deliver partner enablement
territory
The defined geographic or account area covered by rights or activity.define the territory
exclusivity
A restriction or sole right within specified boundaries, subject to agreement and law.assess exclusivity terms
nonexclusive
Not granting a sole right within the stated arrangement.propose a nonexclusive pilot
product scope
The offerings included in the arrangement.specify the product scope
term
The defined duration of an arrangement.agree the pilot term
performance target
A specified level of contribution or result being sought.define performance targets
qualified opportunity
A potential sale meeting the organization's explicit qualification conditions.document qualified opportunities
deal registration
Recording a partner opportunity under the relevant channel process.confirm deal registration
channel conflict
Overlap or disagreement between sales routes or partners.manage channel conflict
attribution rule
A rule defining who receives credit for a contribution or result.agree attribution rules
MDF
Market development funds; resources for specified partner marketing activity under approved terms.request MDF
sell-in
Sales into a channel intermediary, under the relevant measurement definition.measure sell-in
sell-through
Sales onward through the channel to the defined customer group.track sell-through
support handoff
Transfer of responsibility for customer assistance between parties.define the support handoff
renewal review
Assessment before deciding whether to continue an arrangement.schedule a renewal review

Nonexclusive describes proposed rights, not approval of the arrangement. Territory, products, duration, targets, support, funding, and review terms still need agreement. The $5,000 request does not authorize spending on behalf of the seller.

C Notice the language

Partnership language can hide different expectations

Partner may mean joint marketing to one party and exclusive resale rights to another. Name the activities and responsibilities first. Shared ambition does not settle rights, targets, funding, or legal suitability.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the activity
Are you proposing referrals, resale, implementation, or a combination?
Define the request
What territory, products, duration, and contribution would the exclusive right cover?
State authority
I cannot grant exclusivity in this conversation.
Offer a bounded assessment
We can assess a six-month nonexclusive pilot.
State product and territory
The proposal covers the scheduling product in Region North.
Keep the status clear
These are proposed terms, not an executed agreement.
Define contribution
The target is four qualified opportunities per quarter.
Define qualification
Each opportunity needs an identified need, relevant contact, and agreed next step.
Avoid a bookings claim
An opportunity target is not a guarantee of signed orders.
Plan enablement
What training and materials would support the agreed activities?
Handle overlapping accounts
We need rules for registration and channel conflict.
Clarify credit
Agree how joint contributions will be attributed.
Separate the funding request
The five-thousand-dollar marketing request is not approved.
Assign support
Define who handles customer questions and when responsibility transfers.
Set the review
Agree how results and continuation will be assessed at the end of the pilot.
Close responsibly
Submit the full proposal for the required commercial and legal review before promising rights or funding.
6 language and precision notes
Are you proposing
Clarifies the relationship type before discussing rights.
Would cover
Treats exclusivity as a defined proposal rather than a vague privilege.
Can assess
Offers evaluation without granting authority.
Each opportunity
Makes the contribution measure testable.
Not approved
Prevents a funding request becoming implied spending permission.
Full proposal
Keeps rights, obligations, targets, and resources together for review.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response handles exclusivity appropriately?

Answer and explanation

A. Define the scope and contribution, then route the proposal for the required review without promising the right.

Undefined rights need concrete terms and authorized review; enthusiasm alone does not grant exclusivity.

2. Which item meets the supplied qualification definition?

Answer and explanation

B. A prospect with a stated need, relevant contact, and agreed next step

All three stated elements are required. Attendance, an inferred need, or a meeting alone leaves part of the supplied qualification definition unmet.

3. How should the $5,000 be described?

Answer and explanation

A. An unapproved marketing-support request

The brief supplies a request only, without authority to spend, reimburse, or pay it.

4. What should a pilot agreement clarify beyond lead targets?

Answer and explanation

B. Rights, product scope, territory, support roles, attribution, funding, and review

A workable proposal needs the complete relationship boundaries and responsibilities, not a target alone.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Define the partnership before promising exclusivity

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Chen: Potential channel partner / Amina: Channel development manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • performance target
  • nonexclusive
  • qualified opportunity
  • exclusivity
  • channel partner
  • deal registration
  • MDF
  • support handoff
  • attribution rules
  • product scope
  1. Chen
    We could invest in this partnership, but our directors will ask what protects that investment. Would you consider exclusive rights?
  2. Amina
    Let us define the 1 you have in mind. We have not identified the territory, products, duration, or contribution, and I cannot grant an exclusive right in this conversation.
  3. Chen
    We have experience introducing customers and helping with implementation. I have not decided whether we would also handle the commercial sale.
  4. Amina
    Then clarify the 2 activities first. Referrals, resale, and implementation are different roles. The proposal should say which you would perform and where our team would remain responsible.
  5. Chen
    A limited pilot could help us understand the relationship before either side makes a longer commitment.
  6. Amina
    We can assess a 3 six-month pilot. That is a proposal for review, not an executed agreement or a promise that every later commercial request will be accepted.
  7. Chen
    For the pilot, Region North would match our current contacts. We would focus on the scheduling product rather than the full catalog.
  8. Amina
    That makes the 4 and territory more concrete. We should record both, so the pilot is not later described as covering every product or a broader geographic area.
  9. Chen
    What contribution would you expect us to demonstrate during that period? I would prefer a measure we can understand and document.
  10. Amina
    The proposed 5 is four qualified opportunities per quarter. We need a clear definition so the count reflects meaningful customer engagement rather than a list of names.
  11. Chen
    We can identify a customer need, the relevant contact, and an agreed next conversation. That does not mean the customer has decided to buy.
  12. Amina
    Correct. A 6 meets those three conditions in this proposal. It is not a signed order, and we should not turn the target into a promise of bookings or cash receipts.
  13. Chen
    Some prospects may already be talking with your direct sales team. We should avoid approaching them with conflicting offers.
  14. Amina
    We need a 7 process and channel-conflict rules. Agree how overlapping accounts are handled and how each party learns the current status before making commitments to a customer.
  15. Chen
    Joint opportunities will also need a fair way to recognize who contributed. An introduction and implementation support are not the same activity.
  16. Amina
    Define the 8 explicitly. Credit, customer communication, and any compensation should follow the agreed arrangement rather than a general claim that the account belongs to one side forever.
  17. Chen
    We have a five-thousand-dollar launch campaign in mind. Do we need funding approval before booking it, or can we send you the receipts afterward?
  18. Amina
    No. The 9 request is not approved. We need the activity, budget, conditions, and authorization assessed before you assume that our company will fund or reimburse it.
  19. Chen
    Before launch, we also need to know who supports customers and how we review the partnership at the end of the six months.
  20. Amina
    Include the 10, enablement, and renewal-review arrangements in the full proposal. Then the required commercial and legal review can assess the actual relationship before anyone promises rights, spending, or ongoing commitments.

Answers and explanations
  1. exclusivity Turn 2. Exclusivity needs specified boundaries and authorized review; interest in partnering does not create a sole right.
  2. channel partner Turn 4. The channel role may involve introductions, resale, or delivery; those activities carry different responsibilities that need agreement.
  3. nonexclusive Turn 6. Nonexclusive describes the proposed pilot rights; it does not mean the pilot or its other terms are already approved.
  4. product scope Turn 8. The proposed product scope is scheduling only, paired with Region North and the six-month term.
  5. performance target Turn 10. The proposed target is four qualified opportunities per quarter, not a guaranteed number of purchases.
  6. qualified opportunity Turn 12. Qualification here requires need, contact, and an agreed next step; it does not establish an order or revenue.
  7. deal registration Turn 14. Deal registration records partner opportunities under agreed rules so overlapping work and account claims can be handled consistently.
  8. attribution rules Turn 16. Attribution rules establish credit for the actual contributions, rather than allowing competing assumptions about ownership or compensation.
  9. MDF Turn 18. Market development funds are only requested here; the five-thousand amount has not been approved for spending or reimbursement.
  10. support handoff Turn 20. The support handoff defines who assists customers and when responsibility changes, alongside the pilot's review arrangements.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Chen: We could invest in this partnership, but our directors will ask what protects that investment. Would you consider exclusive rights?
  2. Amina: Let us define the exclusivity you have in mind. We have not identified the territory, products, duration, or contribution, and I cannot grant an exclusive right in this conversation.
  3. Chen: We have experience introducing customers and helping with implementation. I have not decided whether we would also handle the commercial sale.
  4. Amina: Then clarify the channel partner activities first. Referrals, resale, and implementation are different roles. The proposal should say which you would perform and where our team would remain responsible.
  5. Chen: A limited pilot could help us understand the relationship before either side makes a longer commitment.
  6. Amina: We can assess a nonexclusive six-month pilot. That is a proposal for review, not an executed agreement or a promise that every later commercial request will be accepted.
  7. Chen: For the pilot, Region North would match our current contacts. We would focus on the scheduling product rather than the full catalog.
  8. Amina: That makes the product scope and territory more concrete. We should record both, so the pilot is not later described as covering every product or a broader geographic area.
  9. Chen: What contribution would you expect us to demonstrate during that period? I would prefer a measure we can understand and document.
  10. Amina: The proposed performance target is four qualified opportunities per quarter. We need a clear definition so the count reflects meaningful customer engagement rather than a list of names.
  11. Chen: We can identify a customer need, the relevant contact, and an agreed next conversation. That does not mean the customer has decided to buy.
  12. Amina: Correct. A qualified opportunity meets those three conditions in this proposal. It is not a signed order, and we should not turn the target into a promise of bookings or cash receipts.
  13. Chen: Some prospects may already be talking with your direct sales team. We should avoid approaching them with conflicting offers.
  14. Amina: We need a deal registration process and channel-conflict rules. Agree how overlapping accounts are handled and how each party learns the current status before making commitments to a customer.
  15. Chen: Joint opportunities will also need a fair way to recognize who contributed. An introduction and implementation support are not the same activity.
  16. Amina: Define the attribution rules explicitly. Credit, customer communication, and any compensation should follow the agreed arrangement rather than a general claim that the account belongs to one side forever.
  17. Chen: We have a five-thousand-dollar launch campaign in mind. Do we need funding approval before booking it, or can we send you the receipts afterward?
  18. Amina: No. The MDF request is not approved. We need the activity, budget, conditions, and authorization assessed before you assume that our company will fund or reimburse it.
  19. Chen: Before launch, we also need to know who supports customers and how we review the partnership at the end of the six months.
  20. Amina: Include the support handoff, enablement, and renewal-review arrangements in the full proposal. Then the required commercial and legal review can assess the actual relationship before anyone promises rights, spending, or ongoing commitments.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Exclusivity needs a defined scope

The lesson's potential channel partner requests exclusivity without specifying territory, activities, or expected contribution.

  1. Potential channel partner: We would like exclusivity before investing time in this partnership. Can you agree in principle?
  2. Partnership manager: We can discuss the request, but territory, activities, and expected contribution haven't been defined.
  3. Potential channel partner: Could we leave those details for later and start with a broad exclusivity commitment?
  4. Partnership manager: That would commit us before the scope is clear. We need a bounded proposal and authorized review.
  5. Potential channel partner: What could we explore while those conditions are being developed?
  6. Partnership manager: A nonexclusive discovery phase or a limited proposal, with any activities and enablement support explicitly agreed.
  7. Potential channel partner: We would need to understand what support is available before estimating our pipeline contribution.
  8. Partnership manager: Agreed. We should compare commitments on both sides rather than assume an exclusivity request creates qualified opportunities.
  9. Potential channel partner: I'll clarify the territory and activities we want to discuss, without treating this conversation as an agreement.
  10. Partnership manager: I'll assess the support requirements and approval route, then return with conditions for a substantive negotiation.
Training support before a reseller launch

Separate fictional case: a prospective reseller requests product training for twenty staff before signing an agreement; training capacity and commercial terms are not confirmed.

  1. Prospective reseller lead: Could you train twenty of our staff before we sign? It would help us prepare the channel launch.
  2. Channel development manager: We can assess enablement support, but training capacity and the commercial arrangement are not yet confirmed.
  3. Prospective reseller lead: Would one introductory session be possible while we discuss a larger training program?
  4. Channel development manager: That's a bounded option to review, with audience, purpose, and scheduling conditions made explicit.
  5. Prospective reseller lead: We don't want an introduction presented as certification or permission to make technical commitments to customers.
  6. Channel development manager: Agreed. The session's scope should distinguish product orientation from any later qualification or authorization.
  7. Prospective reseller lead: Please explain what preparation our staff would need and whether the session depends on an agreement.
  8. Channel development manager: I'll confirm those conditions through the relevant owners rather than invent a training policy here.
  9. Prospective reseller lead: Then we'll hold off announcing a launch date until the partnership and support arrangements are clearer.
  10. Channel development manager: Thank you. I'll bring a reviewed introductory option without promising the full twenty-person program.
A shared lead needs agreed responsibilities

Separate fictional case: two potential partners discuss a jointly identified prospect; neither has agreed customer-contact ownership or pipeline attribution.

  1. Partner account lead: We both identified this prospect. Could we agree who contacts them before we make separate approaches?
  2. Channel manager: Yes. Customer-contact ownership and pipeline attribution are still open, so neither team should assume an exclusive claim.
  3. Partner account lead: We can lead the introduction if your team supports the product discussion, subject to an agreed arrangement.
  4. Channel manager: That is a possible division of work. We need to clarify support, information sharing, and each team's authority.
  5. Partner account lead: Would leading the introduction automatically give us ownership of all later opportunities at the account?
  6. Channel manager: We haven't agreed that. Keep this proposed arrangement bounded to the specific prospect discussion.
  7. Partner account lead: Understood. Let's negotiate this initial activity rather than create account-wide exclusivity by implication.
  8. Channel manager: Agreed. I'll seek the relevant approval and confirm what customer information can be shared through our process.
  9. Partner account lead: I'll pause outreach until we confirm the contact plan, so the customer doesn't receive conflicting approaches.
  10. Channel manager: Then we can proceed with clear responsibilities and record the pipeline status without duplicate ownership claims.

Two scenarios to practice

Define the partnership before promising exclusivity

Describe another proposed channel pilot

A proposed pilot is nonexclusive, lasts four months, covers the inventory product in Region West, and targets three qualified opportunities per quarter. No agreement or marketing funding has been approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The proposed rights are ___."

Answer and explanation

C. nonexclusive

The proposal does not grant a sole selling right in the stated pilot arrangement.

2. Partner: "The proposed term is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. four months

Four months defines the proposed pilot duration, not a permanent appointment.

3. Manager: "The product scope is the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. inventory product

The proposal names one offering rather than the entire catalog.

4. Partner: "The quarterly opportunity target is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. three

Three qualified opportunities is the proposed contribution target, not a guarantee of signed orders.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 07: Partnerships and Channel Development
Original fictional case: Chen requests exclusivity without defining territory, products, duration, or contribution. Amina cannot grant it. For assessment, the team proposes a six-month nonexclusive scheduling-product pilot in fictional Region North, targeting four qualified opportunities per quarter. Qualification requires a customer need, relevant contact, and agreed next step, not an order. Chen requests $5,000 marketing support; none is approved. All pilot terms require commercial and legal review.
Language workshop: Define the partnership before promising exclusivity
Communication goal: Define territory, products, duration, contribution, and funding before promising partnership rights.
Language guidance: A qualified opportunity is not a signed order. This pilot proposal uses three explicit conditions: identified need, relevant contact, and agreed next step. Four opportunities per quarter would measure pipeline contribution under that definition, not guaranteed bookings or cash receipts.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Are you proposing referrals, resale, implementation, or a combination? / What territory, products, duration, and contribution would the exclusive right cover? / I cannot grant exclusivity in this conversation. / We can assess a six-month nonexclusive pilot. / The proposal covers the scheduling product in Region North. / These are proposed terms, not an executed agreement. / The target is four qualified opportunities per quarter. / Each opportunity needs an identified need, relevant contact, and agreed next step. / An opportunity target is not a guarantee of signed orders. / What training and materials would support the agreed activities? / We need rules for registration and channel conflict. / Agree how joint contributions will be attributed. / The five-thousand-dollar marketing request is not approved. / Define who handles customer questions and when responsibility transfers. / Agree how results and continuation will be assessed at the end of the pilot. / Submit the full proposal for the required commercial and legal review before promising rights or funding.
Vocabulary: channel partner: An organization helping market, sell, deliver, or support an offering under agreed terms. / referral partner: A partner introducing potential customers under the relevant arrangement. / reseller: A party selling another organization's offering under agreed rights and terms. / distributor: An intermediary supplying products to other sellers or channels under an arrangement. / VAR: Value-added reseller; a reseller combining an offering with additional services or capabilities. / MSP: Managed service provider; a provider operating specified services for customers. / ISV: Independent software vendor; a company developing and selling its own software. / co-selling: Seller and partner working together on a defined sales opportunity. / partner enablement: Training, resources, and support preparing partners to perform agreed activities. / territory: The defined geographic or account area covered by rights or activity. / exclusivity: A restriction or sole right within specified boundaries, subject to agreement and law. / nonexclusive: Not granting a sole right within the stated arrangement. / product scope: The offerings included in the arrangement. / term: The defined duration of an arrangement. / performance target: A specified level of contribution or result being sought. / qualified opportunity: A potential sale meeting the organization's explicit qualification conditions. / deal registration: Recording a partner opportunity under the relevant channel process. / channel conflict: Overlap or disagreement between sales routes or partners. / attribution rule: A rule defining who receives credit for a contribution or result. / MDF: Market development funds; resources for specified partner marketing activity under approved terms. / sell-in: Sales into a channel intermediary, under the relevant measurement definition. / sell-through: Sales onward through the channel to the defined customer group. / support handoff: Transfer of responsibility for customer assistance between parties. / renewal review: Assessment before deciding whether to continue an arrangement.
Speaking task: Read Chen and Amina aloud, then swap roles. Clarify referrals, resale, and implementation before discussing exclusive rights. Check all ten gaps. Reread the six-month, Region North, scheduling-product proposal and its four-opportunity quarterly target. Repeat the full-proposal close with support, attribution, marketing funding, and review arrangements. Keep all proposed rights and spending unapproved.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08CRM Hygiene and Pipeline ReviewsThe forecast date is the seller estimate, not a customer promise+

Your goal

Correct stages, dates, amounts, and forecast confidence using customer evidence rather than seller hopes.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

The forecast date is the seller estimate, not a customer promise

Opportunity H27 concerns a $120,000 twelve-month subscription plus a separate $20,000 setup fee. Seller Mika records 31 October as the close date, selects Contracting, and puts the deal in Commit. The customer has attended a demonstration and expressed interest, but procurement has not started and no signing date is confirmed. Under this fictional team policy, Contracting requires procurement to have started; Commit requires a customer-confirmed signing window and a documented approval path. Evaluation fits the demonstrated current status. Manager Jonas asks Mika to correct the record and verify buying steps with the customer by Monday at 14:00 Eastern.

Mika: Account executive / Jonas: Sales manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who supplied the 31 October close date?

Answer and explanation

C. Mika entered it as a seller estimate.

The date is seller-entered and has no customer-confirmed signing basis.

2. Why is Contracting unsupported under the stated policy?

Answer and explanation

C. Procurement has not started.

This team's Contracting condition requires procurement to have begun, which the supplied facts say has not happened.

3. What is Mika required to do by Monday at 14:00 Eastern?

Answer and explanation

B. Verify the buying steps with the customer

The follow-up is to establish the customer process, not to promise an unsupported commercial or accounting outcome.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The $120,000 subscription and $20,000 setup fee total $140,000 before any unmentioned taxes or adjustments, but they are different charge types. A forecast field must identify what it includes. None of these quoted amounts is automatically a booking, cash receipt, or recognized revenue.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
CRM
Customer relationship management; systems and practices for maintaining customer and opportunity information.update the CRM
opportunity
A potential sale tracked under the team's defined criteria.maintain the opportunity record
stage exit criterion
Evidence required before an opportunity moves beyond a defined sales stage.verify stage exit criteria
forecast category
A grouping indicating how a deal is treated in the forecast.review the forecast category
Commit
A team's high-confidence forecast category, whose specific requirements must be defined.substantiate Commit status
best case
A forecast scenario or category for possible outcomes under the team's rules.qualify the best-case estimate
pipeline
The set of tracked potential sales and their progress.review the pipeline
pipeline coverage
Potential pipeline value relative to a defined sales target or other stated basis.define pipeline coverage
weighted pipeline
Opportunity values adjusted by assigned probabilities under a specified model.interpret weighted pipeline
close date
The recorded expected or actual completion date, with its status specified.verify the close date
buyer-confirmed date
A date explicitly confirmed by the relevant customer role for the stated event.record a buyer-confirmed date
stage age
Time an opportunity has spent in its current stage.monitor stage age
sales velocity
A defined measure of how quickly sales opportunities generate results.define sales velocity
slippage
Movement of an expected completion beyond the previously forecast period.explain forecast slippage
stale opportunity
A deal record lacking current evidence or meaningful progress under the team's criteria.review stale opportunities
next-step owner
The person responsible for a specified follow-up action.identify the next-step owner
loss reason
The documented reason an opportunity did not proceed, where known.record the loss reason
Closed Won
A completed-sale status used according to the organization's defined completion conditions.verify Closed Won criteria
bookings
Contracted sales value measured under the organization's specified rules.distinguish bookings
recognized revenue
Revenue recorded under the applicable accounting rules, distinct from forecasts or cash receipts.separate recognized revenue
audit trail
A record of changes, sources, and decisions over time.preserve the audit trail
forecast snapshot
A dated record of the forecast at a particular point.retain a forecast snapshot
mutual action plan
Agreed customer and seller steps, owners, and timing.validate the mutual action plan
recurring value
Value from repeating charges, with term and measurement rules specified.define recurring value

Contracting and Commit have explicit fictional requirements in this lesson. Other organizations may define their categories differently. Here the missing procurement start, confirmed signing window, and approval path require correction; they do not prove that the customer will never buy.

C Notice the language

A forecast correction is useful information

Sales pressure can make an evidence-based downgrade feel like personal failure. Treat the correction as a way to expose the next customer task and improve planning. Do not punish honesty by demanding an invented date or confuse removal from a commitment category with abandonment of the opportunity.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the evidence
The customer attended the demonstration and expressed interest.
Identify the unsupported date
The thirty-first of October is our estimate, not a confirmed signing date.
Check the stage
Procurement has not started, so Contracting does not meet our stated rule.
Use the supported stage
Evaluation reflects the evidence currently available.
Check the category
Commit requires a confirmed signing window and a documented approval path.
Avoid converting enthusiasm
A positive comment is not a purchase commitment.
Preserve the opportunity
Removing it from Commit does not mean marking it lost.
Clarify the amount
Separate the twelve-month subscription from the one-time setup fee.
Avoid revenue confusion
A quote in the pipeline is not recognized revenue.
Name the missing customer action
We need to verify procurement steps, approvers, and timing.
Assign ownership
Mika owns the customer follow-up.
Set the deadline
Verify the buying steps by Monday at fourteen hundred Eastern.
Label estimates honestly
Any retained internal target must remain clearly marked as unconfirmed.
Keep history
Record why the stage and forecast treatment changed.
Use evidence in the next review
Bring the customer's actual response, including any remaining unknowns.
Close the correction
Update the stage, forecast category, amount basis, next step, owner, and evidence date.
6 language and precision notes
Our estimate
Attributes the date to the seller rather than the buyer.
Under our rule
Refers to this team's defined category requirements.
Currently available
Leaves room for later evidence without inventing it now.
Does not mean lost
Separates forecast confidence from the opportunity's continued existence.
Amount basis
States which charges and period a field includes.
Actual response
Keeps the follow-up tied to customer evidence rather than internal optimism.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which correction fits the stated policy?

Answer and explanation

C. Move to Evaluation and remove unsupported Commit treatment while keeping the opportunity active.

Evaluation matches the known progress, while the missing approval and timing evidence fails the defined Commit conditions.

2. Which amount statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. The quote has $120,000 subscription plus $20,000 setup; the recorded field must state its basis.

Charge types and field definitions must remain clear, and a quote does not establish completed sales or accounting revenue.

3. What should happen to an internal target date?

Answer and explanation

A. Label it as an unconfirmed estimate and distinguish it from a customer commitment.

An internal planning estimate may be recorded as such, but its source and uncertainty must not be disguised.

4. Which next update is useful?

Answer and explanation

B. Customer-confirmed buying steps and remaining gaps, with the evidence date

The next review needs actual process evidence rather than a revised number unsupported by customer progress.

Answers and explanations
  1. close date Turn 2. The close date is Mika's estimate, not a signing date confirmed by the customer.
  2. stage exit criteria Turn 4. The team requires procurement to have started before Contracting; the known demonstration status fits Evaluation instead.
  3. forecast category Turn 6. The forecast category must reflect the stated evidence requirements; positive feedback does not support Commit here.
  4. pipeline Turn 8. The pipeline can retain a live opportunity without treating it as committed or completed revenue.
  5. recurring value Turn 10. Recurring subscription value is distinct from the one-time setup charge; the forecast field needs a clear inclusion basis.
  6. Recognized revenue Turn 12. Recognized revenue follows applicable accounting rules and is not established by a quote, forecast category, or positive customer comment.
  7. mutual action plan Turn 14. A mutual action plan requires agreement on actual customer and seller steps, not a timetable imposed by the seller.
  8. next-step owner Turn 16. Mika owns the customer follow-up by the stated deadline; that action does not guarantee a signed purchase.
  9. audit trail Turn 18. The audit trail records the evidence-based correction and its reason, rather than rewriting history to imply earlier customer confirmation.
  10. forecast snapshot Turn 20. A forecast snapshot preserves what was estimated at a particular time, including corrections and uncertainty rather than invented certainty.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A close date without customer confirmation

The lesson's deal is forecast to close this month, but procurement has not begun and the customer has given no confirmed date.

  1. Sales manager: The CRM shows a close date this month. What customer evidence supports that date?
  2. Account executive: Procurement hasn't started, and the customer hasn't confirmed a date. The entry reflects our expectation, not their commitment.
  3. Sales manager: Then we should distinguish the internal target from a verified step in the buying process.
  4. Account executive: Agreed. I shouldn't describe the deal as commit solely because the calendar contains a close date.
  5. Sales manager: What should change in the pipeline review while those dependencies remain unresolved?
  6. Account executive: I'll state the missing procurement step and date confirmation, then review the forecast category under our actual definitions.
  7. Sales manager: Good. Don't invent a later customer date simply to move the uncertainty into another month.
  8. Account executive: I'll record the uncertainty and ask the customer about the remaining process and realistic timing.
  9. Sales manager: That gives us evidence to assess possible slippage without claiming the deal is lost or guaranteed.
  10. Account executive: I'll update the record with confirmed information and keep assumptions visibly separate from customer statements.
A verbal preference is not a signed order

Separate fictional case: a prospect says the supplier is its preferred option, but contract review and signature authority remain unconfirmed.

  1. Account executive: The prospect called us their preferred option. I was going to mark the opportunity as effectively closed.
  2. Pipeline reviewer: Preference is positive evidence, but contract review and signature authority remain unconfirmed in the record.
  3. Account executive: Then I should preserve the customer's words without translating them into a purchase commitment.
  4. Pipeline reviewer: Exactly. The CRM should distinguish expressed preference from the steps required for an actual order.
  5. Account executive: Could I keep the current close date as a target while noting that the customer hasn't confirmed it?
  6. Pipeline reviewer: Use the applicable forecast definitions and label its basis clearly. Don't imply a confirmed customer date.
  7. Account executive: I'll ask about the outstanding review and authorized signatory rather than assume our contact can sign.
  8. Pipeline reviewer: Good. A forecast category should reflect the relevant evidence, not simply the optimism of the latest conversation.
  9. Account executive: I'll update the next action and open dependencies, with the preference statement attributed to the prospect.
  10. Pipeline reviewer: That makes the opportunity easier to assess without overstating either certainty or risk.
Two records may describe one opportunity

Separate fictional case: a CRM pipeline contains two records for the same prospect discussion, each with the same proposed value; ownership and duplication have not yet been reconciled.

  1. Revenue operations analyst: These two CRM records appear to describe the same prospect discussion, each carrying the full proposed value.
  2. Sales team lead: Can we include both until ownership is settled, since two account representatives are involved?
  3. Revenue operations analyst: That may double-count one opportunity. Representative involvement doesn't establish two separate purchases.
  4. Sales team lead: Then we need to verify whether the records concern distinct scopes or an actual duplicate.
  5. Revenue operations analyst: Exactly. We shouldn't delete or merge them blindly, but the possible duplication must be visible in the forecast.
  6. Sales team lead: I'll ask both owners to confirm the customer need, scope, and current buying-process evidence.
  7. Revenue operations analyst: Good. The pipeline total should not present an unresolved duplicate as confirmed additional value.
  8. Sales team lead: And we should preserve the relevant history when the records are reconciled under our CRM process.
  9. Revenue operations analyst: Agreed. I'll flag the counting issue and keep the correction traceable rather than silently changing the total.
  10. Sales team lead: I'll coordinate the ownership check and return with evidence of one opportunity or two genuinely distinct scopes.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The forecast date is the seller estimate, not a customer promise
  1. Read Jonas and Mika aloud, then swap roles. State which evidence supports Evaluation and which evidence is missing for Commit.
  2. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the recurring subscription, setup fee, and recognized-revenue distinctions.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct another unsupported forecast

A seller enters 30 November as a close date after an encouraging demonstration. The buyer has not confirmed the date, and required approval steps are unknown. The team needs customer evidence before treating the deal as committed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The entered date is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 30 November

This is the seller-entered date in the record, not an independently confirmed customer commitment.

2. Seller: "The customer has attended a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. demonstration

The demonstration is the stated progress evidence, not a signed or approved purchase.

3. Manager: "The date's current status is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unconfirmed

The buyer has not confirmed the date, so it must remain an estimate.

4. Seller: "Before commitment treatment, we need ___."

Answer and explanation

A. customer evidence

The team's requirement is evidence of the buying process and timing, not enthusiasm alone.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 08: CRM Hygiene and Pipeline Reviews
Original fictional case: Opportunity H27 concerns a $120,000 twelve-month subscription plus a separate $20,000 setup fee. Seller Mika records 31 October as the close date, selects Contracting, and puts the deal in Commit. The customer has attended a demonstration and expressed interest, but procurement has not started and no signing date is confirmed. Under this fictional team policy, Contracting requires procurement to have started; Commit requires a customer-confirmed signing window and a documented approval path. Evaluation fits the demonstrated current status. Manager Jonas asks Mika to correct the record and verify buying steps with the customer by Monday at 14:00 Eastern.
Language workshop: The forecast date is the seller estimate, not a customer promise
Communication goal: Correct stages, dates, amounts, and forecast confidence using customer evidence rather than seller hopes.
Language guidance: The $120,000 subscription and $20,000 setup fee total $140,000 before any unmentioned taxes or adjustments, but they are different charge types. A forecast field must identify what it includes. None of these quoted amounts is automatically a booking, cash receipt, or recognized revenue.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The customer attended the demonstration and expressed interest. / The thirty-first of October is our estimate, not a confirmed signing date. / Procurement has not started, so Contracting does not meet our stated rule. / Evaluation reflects the evidence currently available. / Commit requires a confirmed signing window and a documented approval path. / A positive comment is not a purchase commitment. / Removing it from Commit does not mean marking it lost. / Separate the twelve-month subscription from the one-time setup fee. / A quote in the pipeline is not recognized revenue. / We need to verify procurement steps, approvers, and timing. / Mika owns the customer follow-up. / Verify the buying steps by Monday at fourteen hundred Eastern. / Any retained internal target must remain clearly marked as unconfirmed. / Record why the stage and forecast treatment changed. / Bring the customer's actual response, including any remaining unknowns. / Update the stage, forecast category, amount basis, next step, owner, and evidence date.
Vocabulary: CRM: Customer relationship management; systems and practices for maintaining customer and opportunity information. / opportunity: A potential sale tracked under the team's defined criteria. / stage exit criterion: Evidence required before an opportunity moves beyond a defined sales stage. / forecast category: A grouping indicating how a deal is treated in the forecast. / Commit: A team's high-confidence forecast category, whose specific requirements must be defined. / best case: A forecast scenario or category for possible outcomes under the team's rules. / pipeline: The set of tracked potential sales and their progress. / pipeline coverage: Potential pipeline value relative to a defined sales target or other stated basis. / weighted pipeline: Opportunity values adjusted by assigned probabilities under a specified model. / close date: The recorded expected or actual completion date, with its status specified. / buyer-confirmed date: A date explicitly confirmed by the relevant customer role for the stated event. / stage age: Time an opportunity has spent in its current stage. / sales velocity: A defined measure of how quickly sales opportunities generate results. / slippage: Movement of an expected completion beyond the previously forecast period. / stale opportunity: A deal record lacking current evidence or meaningful progress under the team's criteria. / next-step owner: The person responsible for a specified follow-up action. / loss reason: The documented reason an opportunity did not proceed, where known. / Closed Won: A completed-sale status used according to the organization's defined completion conditions. / bookings: Contracted sales value measured under the organization's specified rules. / recognized revenue: Revenue recorded under the applicable accounting rules, distinct from forecasts or cash receipts. / audit trail: A record of changes, sources, and decisions over time. / forecast snapshot: A dated record of the forecast at a particular point. / mutual action plan: Agreed customer and seller steps, owners, and timing. / recurring value: Value from repeating charges, with term and measurement rules specified.
Speaking task: Read Jonas and Mika aloud, then swap roles. State which evidence supports Evaluation and which evidence is missing for Commit. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the recurring subscription, setup fee, and recognized-revenue distinctions. Repeat Mika's Monday follow-up commitment and correction record without marking the active opportunity lost or attributing the seller's date to the customer.
Partner: Mika: Account executive / Jonas: Sales manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A seller enters 30 November as a close date after an encouraging demonstration. The buyer has not confirmed the date, and required approval steps are unknown. The team needs customer evidence before treating the deal as committed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jonas: Please leave a note explaining the correction. I do not want the next reviewer to read this as a customer withdrawal when we are fixing our own forecast.
Mika: I will preserve the audit trail. The stage and category are changing because the previous entries lacked the required evidence, not because the customer has said it will never buy.
Jonas: At the next review, use the new evidence to reassess confidence. Do not change a probability just to preserve the total.
Mika: I will retain the forecast snapshot with the amount basis, stage, next action, and evidence date. Any internal target will remain unconfirmed, not attributed to the customer.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

The agreement and the release

Confirm quantities and delivery commitments without confusing an annual agreement with an immediate shipment.

Fictional agreement: 1,200 valves over a year, ordered through releases. Release R31 requests 300 by 20 October. Operations can deliver 240 by that date and 60 by 27 October, subject to customer acceptance of the split. The customer needs all 300 by 24 October. No expedited alternative is confirmed. Agreement terms and dates are invented.

Lena: Industrial account manager / Pavel: Customer buyer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • substitution
  • required-on-site date
  • release order
  • delivery commitment
  • expedite review
  • split delivery
  1. Pavel
    We have an annual agreement for twelve hundred valves. My team expects the three hundred on release R31 by October twentieth. Can you confirm that delivery?
  2. Lena
    I checked the 1 with operations. They can deliver two hundred forty by the twentieth and the remaining sixty by the twenty-seventh, if you accept that split.
  3. Pavel
    That is not the date on our release. We need all three hundred for the work, and the latest usable arrival date is the twenty-fourth.
  4. Lena
    Then our proposed 2 does not meet your full requirement. The first batch arrives in time, but the final sixty would be three days too late.
  5. Pavel
    I do not want the annual agreement used as evidence that stock was reserved for this exact date. What does the current promise actually cover?
  6. Lena
    The 3 needs quantities and dates against R31. I will not mark the whole release confirmed for the twentieth when operations has not offered that.
  7. Pavel
    Can another depot supply the sixty? We might accept a separate shipment if it reaches us by the twenty-fourth and the total is still three hundred.
  8. Lena
    I can request an 4. We need actual availability, transit timing, and any extra charge before I offer that route as a solution.
  9. Pavel
    Please distinguish dispatch from arrival. A vehicle leaving on the twenty-fourth would not help if the valves arrive the following day.
  10. Lena
    Understood. Your 5 is the twenty-fourth at the latest. I will give operations the delivery address and receiving constraints, not just a date without an event.
  11. Pavel
    Our receiving team can handle the first two hundred forty on the twentieth. That is useful, but I am not accepting a late balance by saying so.
  12. Lena
    I will record that distinction. Acceptance of one proposed batch does not silently approve the revised date for the balance or change your stated need.
  13. Pavel
    The first batch and the balance must use the same approved valve specification. Please do not substitute a similar part to fill the gap without asking.
  14. Lena
    Any 6 needs the required approval. I will ask about the specified item first and keep any alternative clearly identified as a separate proposal.
  15. Pavel
    How will you update the paperwork if an earlier balance becomes possible? Our planning team will use the confirmation, not this call, to schedule the work.
  16. Lena
    The revised confirmation will identify R31, each quantity, its arrival date, and any approved cost change. It should also state which earlier confirmation it replaces.
  17. Pavel
    And the annual quantity should not become fifteen hundred because someone adds this release to the twelve-hundred agreement as though it were extra business.
  18. Lena
    Correct. These three hundred are ordered against that agreement, leaving nine hundred not yet released on the supplied facts. We must not count the same quantity twice.
  19. Pavel
    Please come back with the expedite result. Until then, our need remains three hundred by the twenty-fourth at the latest, with only the first batch workable as proposed.
  20. Lena
    I will report the confirmed options and remaining shortfall. I will not promise an earlier balance until the supply and delivery checks support it.

Answers and explanations
  1. release order Turn 2. The release order specifies the 300 units requested now; the annual agreement's 1,200-unit commitment is not itself an instruction to ship all units immediately.
  2. split delivery Turn 4. A split delivery supplies one release in separate quantities and arrivals; here the proposed 240 and 60 add to 300, but the second arrival misses the customer's latest usable date.
  3. delivery commitment Turn 6. The delivery commitment must identify the promised quantity and arrival date; the proposed dates have been checked with operations, but the customer has not accepted this revised split.
  4. expedite review Turn 8. An expedite review checks whether a faster supply route is feasible, including stock, transport, cost, and authority; requesting it does not establish that the sixty units can arrive earlier.
  5. required-on-site date Turn 10. The required-on-site date is when the goods must arrive where needed, not when they leave the supplier; the customer's latest usable arrival is 24 October.
  6. substitution Turn 14. A substitution would replace the specified item with another; a supply shortage does not authorize a different valve specification without the customer's required technical and commercial approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Pavel: We have an annual agreement for twelve hundred valves. My team expects the three hundred on release R31 by October twentieth. Can you confirm that delivery?
  2. Lena: I checked the release order with operations. They can deliver two hundred forty by the twentieth and the remaining sixty by the twenty-seventh, if you accept that split.
  3. Pavel: That is not the date on our release. We need all three hundred for the work, and the latest usable arrival date is the twenty-fourth.
  4. Lena: Then our proposed split delivery does not meet your full requirement. The first batch arrives in time, but the final sixty would be three days too late.
  5. Pavel: I do not want the annual agreement used as evidence that stock was reserved for this exact date. What does the current promise actually cover?
  6. Lena: The delivery commitment needs quantities and dates against R31. I will not mark the whole release confirmed for the twentieth when operations has not offered that.
  7. Pavel: Can another depot supply the sixty? We might accept a separate shipment if it reaches us by the twenty-fourth and the total is still three hundred.
  8. Lena: I can request an expedite review. We need actual availability, transit timing, and any extra charge before I offer that route as a solution.
  9. Pavel: Please distinguish dispatch from arrival. A vehicle leaving on the twenty-fourth would not help if the valves arrive the following day.
  10. Lena: Understood. Your required-on-site date is the twenty-fourth at the latest. I will give operations the delivery address and receiving constraints, not just a date without an event.
  11. Pavel: Our receiving team can handle the first two hundred forty on the twentieth. That is useful, but I am not accepting a late balance by saying so.
  12. Lena: I will record that distinction. Acceptance of one proposed batch does not silently approve the revised date for the balance or change your stated need.
  13. Pavel: The first batch and the balance must use the same approved valve specification. Please do not substitute a similar part to fill the gap without asking.
  14. Lena: Any substitution needs the required approval. I will ask about the specified item first and keep any alternative clearly identified as a separate proposal.
  15. Pavel: How will you update the paperwork if an earlier balance becomes possible? Our planning team will use the confirmation, not this call, to schedule the work.
  16. Lena: The revised confirmation will identify R31, each quantity, its arrival date, and any approved cost change. It should also state which earlier confirmation it replaces.
  17. Pavel: And the annual quantity should not become fifteen hundred because someone adds this release to the twelve-hundred agreement as though it were extra business.
  18. Lena: Correct. These three hundred are ordered against that agreement, leaving nine hundred not yet released on the supplied facts. We must not count the same quantity twice.
  19. Pavel: Please come back with the expedite result. Until then, our need remains three hundred by the twenty-fourth at the latest, with only the first batch workable as proposed.
  20. Lena: I will report the confirmed options and remaining shortfall. I will not promise an earlier balance until the supply and delivery checks support it.
Confirm another release and balance

An annual 900-unit agreement has one 250-unit release. The supplier proposes arrival of 200 on 12 May and 50 on 19 May. The customer needs all units by 16 May. No expedited balance is confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Seller: The quantity not yet released under the annual agreement is ___.

Answer and explanation

B. 650

Nine hundred committed annually minus the single two-hundred-fifty release leaves six hundred fifty not yet released.

2. Buyer: The quantity arriving in time under the proposal is ___.

Answer and explanation

D. 200

The first two hundred arrive on 12 May, before the latest required arrival on 16 May.

3. Seller: The late balance is ___ units.

Answer and explanation

B. 50

The remaining fifty would arrive on 19 May, three days after the customer's latest usable arrival.

4. Buyer: A faster balance is still ___.

Answer and explanation

D. unconfirmed

The supplied facts contain no verified expedited option, so a request to check it is not a delivery promise.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
